Hi, doctor. Well, I guess I'm mostly here because for a really long time now, my sleep has just been completely broken. I mean, I fall into bed absolutely exhausted—I'm a home health aide, so I'm on my feet and helping lift people all day. But then I just wake up to every little sound in the house. 

Like the pipes. The plumbing in my house is so old, it makes this awful clanking noise. My client Mrs. Davis has the same issue at her place, actually. I was telling her son just last week that he really needs to get a plumber out there because it's not safe. Especially with her using a walker, if there's a leak and a puddle... she tripped last month, though thank goodness she didn't break her hip. They really need to fix that. 

But, um... where was I? Oh, right, my sleep. Yeah, so every little noise wakes me up, and then I'm just up way before my alarm even goes off. I start my day feeling like I never really rested at all.

Um, how long it takes? I'm not really sure, honestly. I don't really look at the clock. I usually just collapse into bed and turn the TV on for a bit. I was watching this show last night, actually, about these people fixing up old cabins in Maine. It looks so beautiful up there. My sister went to Maine for her honeymoon, oh, gosh, it must have been twenty years ago now. They ate lobster every single day. I'm allergic to shellfish, though, so I'd probably just have to eat blueberry pancakes if I ever went. They say Maine has the best blueberries. I usually just buy the frozen ones from the grocery store to put in my oatmeal before work. It's easier that way, you know? 

But anyway, yeah, I don't know exactly how many minutes it takes me to drift off. I just know I don't stay asleep. Like I said, the pipes... or a car driving by, or just nothing at all. It's always something, and then I'm just lying there awake in the dark.

Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying earlier, my sleep is just so broken. Even if I've been in bed for hours, I'm always up before the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really rested at all.

Her mother was so worried about her living on campus, but I told her she'd be just fine. \n\nBut, um... yeah, even before the radio starts yelling, I'm already awake in the dark, and I just start my day feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day, even after a full night’s rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like this deep, heavy feeling all the time. By the time I finish seeing my home-health clients—and some of them need a lot of physical help, you know, transferring from the bed to the chair and all that. Mrs. Davis, the one I mentioned before, she actually has this really nice lift chair now. Her son finally bought it for her. It's one of those plush, brown microfiber ones. It matches her sofa perfectly, which is funny because she bought that sofa at a Sears back in the nineties. You can't even find a Sears anymore, can you? I used to love going there for the Kenmore appliances. My mother had a Kenmore washing machine that lasted twenty-five years. They just don't make things like they used to. \n\nBut anyway, by the time I get home from work and try to help out around my own house, my body just feels so incredibly heavy. Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / home_health_aide / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I feel like the color has gone out of things, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family or looking at art I used to enjoy, and I have to work hard not to tear up for no clear reason.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. 

But anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.

Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the "L", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. 

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.

Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you really liked before?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / home_health_aide / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I sort of just talked about that, didn't I? With the art books and the dinners. It's like I was saying, I just don't feel that connection to the joy of it anymore. My sister, she used to tell me I was too obsessed with those paintings anyway. She's more into practical things, you know? She makes these incredible quilts. Hand-stitched, every single one. She entered one in the county fair last summer—this beautiful blue and white star pattern—and won a ribbon. The fair was so crowded that day, though. We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. They were those orthopedic ones, really expensive, but you need them when you're on your feet all day like I am with my clients. 

But yeah, to answer your question... I guess I already did. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. 

But anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.

really is most of the day, almost every single day. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like you're in a fog or everything is in slow motion?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or anything like that. If anything, I'm just tired from work, but I'm moving at my normal pace. I mean, you have to when you're doing home visits. You're on a strict schedule. If I'm late to see Mr. Abernathy, he gets so agitated. He sits by the front window with his pocket watch—an actual gold pocket watch that belonged to his grandfather, can you believe it? He told me once it survived the trenches in World War I. He polished it so much the engraving on the back is almost completely worn off. You can barely make out the initials anymore. It's a shame, really, because things like that, they're family history. My grandfather had a beautiful old clock on his mantel, one of those ones that chimes every hour. It used to drive my grandmother absolutely crazy when she was trying to watch her soap operas. She never missed an episode of Days of Our Lives. 

But um, no, I'm not moving in slow motion or in a fog or anything. Just the normal kind of tired.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today? How can I help you?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I guess I'm mostly here because for a really long time now, my sleep has just been completely broken. I mean, I fall into bed absolutely exhausted—I'm a home health aide, so I'm on my feet and helping lift people all day. But then I just wake up to every little sound in the house. \n\nLike the pipes. The plumbing in my house is so old, it makes this awful clanking noise. My client Mrs. Davis has the same issue at her place, actually. I was telling her son just last week that he really needs to get a plumber out there because it's not safe. Especially with her using a walker, if there's a leak and a puddle... she tripped last month, though thank goodness she didn't break her hip. They really need to fix that. \n\nBut, um... where was I? Oh, right, my sleep. Yeah, so every little noise wakes me up, and then I'm just up way before my alarm even goes off. I start my day feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how long it takes you to fall asleep and if you have trouble staying asleep throughout the night?\n\nPatient: Um, how long it takes? I'm not really sure, honestly. I don't really look at the clock. I usually just collapse into bed and turn the TV on for a bit. I was watching this show last night, actually, about these people fixing up old cabins in Maine. It looks so beautiful up there. My sister went to Maine for her honeymoon, oh, gosh, it must have been twenty years ago now. They ate lobster every single day. I'm allergic to shellfish, though, so I'd probably just have to eat blueberry pancakes if I ever went. They say Maine has the best blueberries. I usually just buy the frozen ones from the grocery store to put in my oatmeal before work. It's easier that way, you know? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't know exactly how many minutes it takes me to drift off. I just know I don't stay asleep. Like I said, the pipes... or a car driving by, or just nothing at all. It's always something, and then I'm just lying there awake in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you haven't rested well, even if you think you've slept enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying earlier, my sleep is just so broken. Even if I've been in bed for hours, I'm always up before the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really rested at all. \n\nSpeaking of alarms, I have this old radio one, and the station I used to wake up to just changed formats. It used to be soft rock, which was nice and peaceful, but now it's all talk radio. It's just people yelling at each other at five in the morning! I really need to get one of those clocks that lights up like the sunrise instead. My niece got one of those for her dorm room—she just started at the state college, studying physical therapy. We're all so proud of her, she's incredibly smart. Her mother was so worried about her living on campus, but I told her she'd be just fine. \n\nBut, um... yeah, even before the radio starts yelling, I'm already awake in the dark, and I just start my day feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day, even after a full night’s rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like this deep, heavy feeling all the time. By the time I finish seeing my home-health clients—and some of them need a lot of physical help, you know, transferring from the bed to the chair and all that. Mrs. Davis, the one I mentioned before, she actually has this really nice lift chair now. Her son finally bought it for her. It's one of those plush, brown microfiber ones. It matches her sofa perfectly, which is funny because she bought that sofa at a Sears back in the nineties. You can't even find a Sears anymore, can you? I used to love going there for the Kenmore appliances. My mother had a Kenmore washing machine that lasted twenty-five years. They just don't make things like they used to. \n\nBut anyway, by the time I get home from work and try to help out around my own house, my body just feels so incredibly heavy. Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you really liked before?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I sort of just talked about that, didn't I? With the art books and the dinners. It's like I was saying, I just don't feel that connection to the joy of it anymore. My sister, she used to tell me I was too obsessed with those paintings anyway. She's more into practical things, you know? She makes these incredible quilts. Hand-stitched, every single one. She entered one in the county fair last summer—this beautiful blue and white star pattern—and won a ribbon. The fair was so crowded that day, though. We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. They were those orthopedic ones, really expensive, but you need them when you're on your feet all day like I am with my clients. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question... I guess I already did. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like you're in a fog or everything is in slow motion?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or anything like that. If anything, I'm just tired from work, but I'm moving at my normal pace. I mean, you have to when you're doing home visits. You're on a strict schedule. If I'm late to see Mr. Abernathy, he gets so agitated. He sits by the front window with his pocket watch—an actual gold pocket watch that belonged to his grandfather, can you believe it? It's a shame, really, because things like that, they're family history. My grandfather had a beautiful old clock on his mantel, one of those ones that chimes every hour. It used to drive my grandmother absolutely crazy when she was trying to watch her soap operas. She never missed an episode of Days of Our Lives. \n\nBut um, no, I'm not moving in slow motion or in a fog or anything. Just the normal kind of tired.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Her mother was so worried about her living on campus, but I told her she'd be just fine. \n\nBut, um... yeah, even before the radio starts yelling, I'm already awake in the dark, and I just start my day feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day, even after a full night’s rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like this deep, heavy feeling all the time. By the time I finish seeing my home-health clients—and some of them need a lot of physical help, you know, transferring from the bed to the chair and all that. Mrs. Davis, the one I mentioned before, she actually has this really nice lift chair now. Her son finally bought it for her. It's one of those plush, brown microfiber ones. It matches her sofa perfectly, which is funny because she bought that sofa at a Sears back in the nineties. You can't even find a Sears anymore, can you? I used to love going there for the Kenmore appliances. My mother had a Kenmore washing machine that lasted twenty-five years. They just don't make things like they used to. \n\nBut anyway, by the time I get home from work and try to help out around my own house, my body just feels so incredibly heavy. Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you really liked before?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I sort of just talked about that, didn't I? With the art books and the dinners. It's like I was saying, I just don't feel that connection to the joy of it anymore. My sister, she used to tell me I was too obsessed with those paintings anyway. She's more into practical things, you know? She makes these incredible quilts. Hand-stitched, every single one. She entered one in the county fair last summer—this beautiful blue and white star pattern—and won a ribbon. The fair was so crowded that day, though. We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. They were those orthopedic ones, really expensive, but you need them when you're on your feet all day like I am with my clients. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question... I guess I already did. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like you're in a fog or everything is in slow motion?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or anything like that. If anything, I'm just tired from work, but I'm moving at my normal pace. I mean, you have to when you're doing home visits. You're on a strict schedule. If I'm late to see Mr. Abernathy, he gets so agitated. He sits by the front window with his pocket watch—an actual gold pocket watch that belonged to his grandfather, can you believe it? He told me once it survived the trenches in World War I. He polished it so much the engraving on the back is almost completely worn off. You can barely make out the initials anymore. It's a shame, really, because things like that, they're family history. My grandfather had a beautiful old clock on his mantel, one of those ones that chimes every hour. It used to drive my grandmother absolutely crazy when she was trying to watch her soap operas. She never missed an episode of Days of Our Lives. \n\nBut um, no, I'm not moving in slow motion or in a fog or anything. Just the normal kind of tired.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don't think so. I mean, I've never really felt quite like this before. Everyone gets sad sometimes, right? Like when I was taking classes at the community college years ago, I used to get so down about my grades. I was trying to get my nursing degree, but the anatomy class was just impossible. The professor, Dr. Aris, he had this really thick mustache that covered his whole top lip, and when he talked, you couldn't even see his mouth moving. It was honestly like watching a ventriloquist act. He used to bring in these actual bones for us to memorize. Real human bones! He kept them in this old, scuffed-up leather doctor's bag that looked like it belonged in a museum. I always wondered where he got that bag. Probably at an antique shop. I used to love going to antique shops on the weekends. There's one downtown that sells the most beautiful old stained glass windows. They salvage them from churches that are being torn down. It's such a shame they tear down those beautiful old stone buildings just to put up another strip mall. They just put a new frozen yogurt place in where the old post office used to be, and the parking lot is an absolute nightmare to get in and out of. 

But um... wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about feeling sad before. No, I really don't think I've ever had a time where I felt this flat for this long. This is the first time it's been like this.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today? How can I help you?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I guess I'm mostly here because for a really long time now, my sleep has just been completely broken. I mean, I fall into bed absolutely exhausted—I'm a home health aide, so I'm on my feet and helping lift people all day. But then I just wake up to every little sound in the house. \n\nLike the pipes. The plumbing in my house is so old, it makes this awful clanking noise. My client Mrs. Davis has the same issue at her place, actually. I was telling her son just last week that he really needs to get a plumber out there because it's not safe. Especially with her using a walker, if there's a leak and a puddle... she tripped last month, though thank goodness she didn't break her hip. They really need to fix that. \n\nBut, um... where was I? Oh, right, my sleep. Yeah, so every little noise wakes me up, and then I'm just up way before my alarm even goes off. I start my day feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how long it takes you to fall asleep and if you have trouble staying asleep throughout the night?\n\nPatient: Um, how long it takes? I'm not really sure, honestly. I don't really look at the clock. I usually just collapse into bed and turn the TV on for a bit. I was watching this show last night, actually, about these people fixing up old cabins in Maine. It looks so beautiful up there. My sister went to Maine for her honeymoon, oh, gosh, it must have been twenty years ago now. They ate lobster every single day. I'm allergic to shellfish, though, so I'd probably just have to eat blueberry pancakes if I ever went. They say Maine has the best blueberries. I usually just buy the frozen ones from the grocery store to put in my oatmeal before work. It's easier that way, you know? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't know exactly how many minutes it takes me to drift off. I just know I don't stay asleep. Like I said, the pipes... or a car driving by, or just nothing at all. It's always something, and then I'm just lying there awake in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you haven't rested well, even if you think you've slept enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying earlier, my sleep is just so broken. Even if I've been in bed for hours, I'm always up before the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really rested at all. \n\nSpeaking of alarms, I have this old radio one, and the station I used to wake up to just changed formats. It used to be soft rock, which was nice and peaceful, but now it's all talk radio. It's just people yelling at each other at five in the morning! I really need to get one of those clocks that lights up like the sunrise instead. My niece got one of those for her dorm room—she just started at the state college, studying physical therapy. We're all so proud of her, she's incredibly smart. Her mother was so worried about her living on campus, but I told her she'd be just fine. \n\nBut, um... yeah, even before the radio starts yelling, I'm already awake in the dark, and I just start my day feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day, even after a full night’s rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like this deep, heavy feeling all the time. By the time I finish seeing my home-health clients—and some of them need a lot of physical help, you know, transferring from the bed to the chair and all that. Mrs. Davis, the one I mentioned before, she actually has this really nice lift chair now. Her son finally bought it for her. It's one of those plush, brown microfiber ones. It matches her sofa perfectly, which is funny because she bought that sofa at a Sears back in the nineties. You can't even find a Sears anymore, can you? I used to love going there for the Kenmore appliances. My mother had a Kenmore washing machine that lasted twenty-five years. They just don't make things like they used to. \n\nBut anyway, by the time I get home from work and try to help out around my own house, my body just feels so incredibly heavy. Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you really liked before?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I sort of just talked about that, didn't I? With the art books and the dinners. It's like I was saying, I just don't feel that connection to the joy of it anymore. My sister, she used to tell me I was too obsessed with those paintings anyway. She's more into practical things, you know? She makes these incredible quilts. Hand-stitched, every single one. She entered one in the county fair last summer—this beautiful blue and white star pattern—and won a ribbon. The fair was so crowded that day, though. We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. They were those orthopedic ones, really expensive, but you need them when you're on your feet all day like I am with my clients. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question... I guess I already did. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like you're in a fog or everything is in slow motion?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or anything like that. If anything, I'm just tired from work, but I'm moving at my normal pace. I mean, you have to when you're doing home visits. You're on a strict schedule. If I'm late to see Mr. Abernathy, he gets so agitated. He sits by the front window with his pocket watch—an actual gold pocket watch that belonged to his grandfather, can you believe it? He told me once it survived the trenches in World War I. He polished it so much the engraving on the back is almost completely worn off. You can barely make out the initials anymore. It's a shame, really, because things like that, they're family history. My grandfather had a beautiful old clock on his mantel, one of those ones that chimes every hour. It used to drive my grandmother absolutely crazy when she was trying to watch her soap operas. She never missed an episode of Days of Our Lives. \n\nBut um, no, I'm not moving in slow motion or in a fog or anything. Just the normal kind of tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt persistently sad and lost interest in the things you used to enjoy, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I've never really felt quite like this before. Everyone gets sad sometimes, right? Like when I was taking classes at the community college years ago, I used to get so down about my grades. I was trying to get my nursing degree, but the anatomy class was just impossible. The professor, Dr. Aris, he had this really thick mustache that covered his whole top lip, and when he talked, you couldn't even see his mouth moving. It was honestly like watching a ventriloquist act. He used to bring in these actual bones for us to memorize. Real human bones! He kept them in this old, scuffed-up leather doctor's bag that looked like it belonged in a museum. I always wondered where he got that bag. Probably at an antique shop. I used to love going to antique shops on the weekends. There's one downtown that sells the most beautiful old stained glass windows. They salvage them from churches that are being torn down. It's such a shame they tear down those beautiful old stone buildings just to put up another strip mall. They just put a new frozen yogurt place in where the old post office used to be, and the parking lot is an absolute nightmare to get in and out of. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about feeling sad before. No, I really don't think I've ever had a time where I felt this flat for this long. This is the first time it's been like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It was honestly like watching a ventriloquist act. He used to bring in these actual bones for us to memorize. Real human bones! He kept them in this old, scuffed-up leather doctor's bag that looked like it belonged in a museum. I always wondered where he got that bag. Probably at an antique shop. I used to love going to antique shops on the weekends. There's one downtown that sells the most beautiful old stained glass windows. They salvage them from churches that are being torn down. It's such a shame they tear down those beautiful old stone buildings just to put up another strip mall. They just put a new frozen yogurt place in where the old post office used to be, and the parking lot is an absolute nightmare to get in and out of. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about feeling sad before. No, I really don't think I've ever had a time where I felt this flat for this long. This is the first time it's been like this.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in the past where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for more than two weeks, even if it got better on its own?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / home_health_aide / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, like I was just saying, I really haven't. I mean, two weeks is a long time to feel this way, right? Although, time is so funny lately. It feels like just yesterday I was helping my daughter move into her first apartment. She got this little place over on Elm Street, you know, right above that bakery that makes those incredible sourdough loaves. The smell in the mornings is just heaven. But the stairs up to her place are so steep. I helped her carry this old dresser we found at a yard sale—solid oak, heavy as a boulder. We had to pivot it around the landing and I swear I almost threw my back out. I had to use heating pads for three days after that. My cat, Barnaby, he loved the heating pad. He's this big orange tabby who thinks he owns the house. He'll just come right up and sit on whatever you're trying to use—the newspaper, the heating pad, my lap when I'm trying to fold laundry. He sheds everywhere too. I go through those sticky lint rollers like you wouldn't believe. I buy them in bulk at Costco now. Do you shop at Costco? The rotisserie chickens are such a good deal, but the parking lot on a Saturday... well, you take your life into your own hands. 

But no, to answer your question, I haven't ever had a time where I felt down for weeks on end before this started. This is all new to me.

I was watching this show last night, actually, about these people fixing up old cabins in Maine. It looks so beautiful up there. My sister went to Maine for her honeymoon, oh, gosh, it must have been twenty years ago now. They ate lobster every single day. I usually just buy the frozen ones from the grocery store to put in my oatmeal before work. Like I said, the pipes... or a car driving by, or just nothing at all. Like I was saying earlier, my sleep is just so broken. Even if I've been in bed for hours, I'm always up before the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really rested at all. \n\nSpeaking of alarms, I have this old radio one, and the station I used to wake up to just changed formats. It used to be soft rock, which was nice and peaceful, but now it's all talk radio. It's just people yelling at each other at five in the morning! I really need to get one of those clocks that lights up like the sunrise instead. My niece got one of those for her dorm room—she just started at the state college, studying physical therapy. We're all so proud of her, she's incredibly smart. Her mother was so worried about her living on campus, but I told her she'd be just fine. \n\nBut, um... yeah, even before the radio starts yelling, I'm already awake in the dark, and I just start my day feeling completely exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel excessively tired or fatigued during the day, even after a full night’s rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like this deep, heavy feeling all the time. By the time I finish seeing my home-health clients—and some of them need a lot of physical help, you know, transferring from the bed to the chair and all that. Mrs. Davis, the one I mentioned before, she actually has this really nice lift chair now. Her son finally bought it for her. It's one of those plush, brown microfiber ones. It matches her sofa perfectly, which is funny because she bought that sofa at a Sears back in the nineties. You can't even find a Sears anymore, can you? I used to love going there for the Kenmore appliances. My mother had a Kenmore washing machine that lasted twenty-five years. They just don't make things like they used to. \n\nBut anyway, by the time I get home from work and try to help out around my own house, my body just feels so incredibly heavy. Even just sitting down to chart my notes for the day, or trying to fold a basket of laundry... it just feels like too much to push through. I just have no energy left for anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I have. Most days it just feels like the color has completely gone out of things, you know? Like, even when I'm sitting at Sunday dinner with my family... we usually do a big pot roast on Sundays. Well, lately I've been doing chicken or pork because the price of beef is just outrageous right now. Have you seen what they charge for a chuck roast at the supermarket? It's highway robbery. My mother used to get all her meat from this little family-owned butcher shop on 4th street, but they closed down years ago. Now it's one of those boutique pet bakeries. Can you imagine? Buying gourmet frosted cupcakes for a dog. My neighbor has a little pug that she dresses up in tiny knit sweaters, and she actually buys him those treats for his birthday. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, even at those family dinners, or when I'm looking at art I used to really enjoy, I just feel so flat. It's like I can't connect to the joy of it anymore. And I find myself having to work really hard not to tear up, just out of nowhere, for no clear reason at all. It's just this heavy sadness that won't go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific events?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was just saying... it really is most of the day, almost every single day. It's not just when something bad happens. It's just this constant feeling that the color has drained out of my life. Like I mentioned about Sunday dinners, or even when I try to look at my art books... I used to love flipping through those big, glossy books of Impressionist paintings. Monet, Renoir, all of them. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! I remember wearing a heavy wool coat and still shivering while waiting for the train. They have that wonderful elevated train system, the \"L\", they call it. It’s so noisy, but it’s fascinating how it weaves right between the buildings. I always wondered how people living in those apartments could sleep with the trains rattling by their windows all night. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the sadness. Yeah, it's just there all the time. I'll just be sitting there, looking at those beautiful paintings or sitting with my family, and I have to fight so hard just to keep from crying. And there's no reason for it, no specific event that triggered it. Just... tears welling up, out of nowhere, almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you really liked before?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I sort of just talked about that, didn't I? With the art books and the dinners. It's like I was saying, I just don't feel that connection to the joy of it anymore. My sister, she used to tell me I was too obsessed with those paintings anyway. She's more into practical things, you know? She makes these incredible quilts. Hand-stitched, every single one. She entered one in the county fair last summer—this beautiful blue and white star pattern—and won a ribbon. The fair was so crowded that day, though. We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. They were those orthopedic ones, really expensive, but you need them when you're on your feet all day like I am with my clients. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question... I guess I already did. I just don't get the same feeling from things I used to love. It's all just kind of flat now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, I really just don't have much of an appetite anymore. I end up just picking at whatever I make for everyone else at dinner, or I'll just grab a piece of toast after my shift. I work as a home health aide, you know, and it's exhausting work. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. And I've noticed my clothes have been hanging a lot looser lately, without me even trying to lose weight. I had to pull my belt to the last hole the other day just to keep my work pants up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like you're in a fog or everything is in slow motion?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel slowed down or anything like that. If anything, I'm just tired from work, but I'm moving at my normal pace. I mean, you have to when you're doing home visits. You're on a strict schedule. If I'm late to see Mr. Abernathy, he gets so agitated. He sits by the front window with his pocket watch—an actual gold pocket watch that belonged to his grandfather, can you believe it? He told me once it survived the trenches in World War I. He polished it so much the engraving on the back is almost completely worn off. You can barely make out the initials anymore. It's a shame, really, because things like that, they're family history. My grandfather had a beautiful old clock on his mantel, one of those ones that chimes every hour. It used to drive my grandmother absolutely crazy when she was trying to watch her soap operas. She never missed an episode of Days of Our Lives. \n\nBut um, no, I'm not moving in slow motion or in a fog or anything. Just the normal kind of tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt persistently sad and lost interest in the things you used to enjoy, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. It's such a shame they tear down those beautiful old stone buildings just to put up another strip mall. I bought this beautiful, heavy coffee table book at a museum gift shop on a trip to Chicago years ago. Oh, the wind off the lake there is just freezing, even in the spring! We got stuck parking out in a muddy field, and I completely ruined my good walking shoes. You're constantly lifting people, helping them bathe, making sure they take their pills... Mrs. Higgins, one of my regulars, she has this pill organizer that is just a nightmare. It's got four different slots for every day of the week, and she insists on taking her evening pills with exactly half a glass of lukewarm water. If it's too cold, she complains it hurts her teeth. She has those old dentures, the kind they used to make back in the seventies. I always tell her she should go get them refitted, but she refuses to see the dentist. Says the dentist office smells too much like cloves and it gives her a headache. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... I'm just not eating much. Mostly just the toast. He sheds everywhere too. I go through those sticky lint rollers like you wouldn't believe. I buy them in bulk at Costco now. Do you shop at Costco? The rotisserie chickens are such a good deal, but the parking lot on a Saturday... well, you take your life into your own hands. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't ever had a time where I felt down for weeks on end before this started.
